Keep temp cable runs continuous/unbroken (no in-line splices) and maintain ≥1 m between a temporary bulb socket and any outlet block (heat and cable-tugging risk). Reserve a dedicated ~10A circuit for a job-site charging station. `confirmed`. [source: yt_VT-zl1Xay4A, yt_957RF_DcjwA]
- **Off-the-shelf retail extension cords are commonly undersized for job-site power draw** (this channel cites ~0.75 mm² conductors) — build a job-site extension from proper 2.5 mm² cable instead of relying on a retail cord for sustained tool/heater loads. `confirmed` as the channel's stated practice; no independent verification of the 0.75 mm² figure. [source: yt_VT-zl1Xay4A]
- **Leave 1–2 working temporary outlets plus one working light per room during rough-in**, even on a multi-floor build before any finish work starts — improves site usability/safety during the build itself. `single-account`, thin technical detail (customer-testimonial-style source). [source: yt_9L9xTWQLZZ8]

**Switches & controls**

- **All standard toggle-style switches in an apartment should share the same ON/OFF orientation** (e.g. down = on, consistently) — a mixed-orientation install is a common, avoidable annoyance. Prefer several single-gang switches over one crowded multi-gang switch plate where space allows. Never position a switch inside a future door's swing zone (checked against the door leaf's full opening arc, not just its closed position). `confirmed`. [source: yt_8eECI5sWEy4]
- **Two-way switches work in pairs; a three-or-more-point control run adds "intermediate" (crossover/перекрёстный) switches between the two end switches**, not more two-way switches — a distinct component from a standard two-way switch. Wire joints must terminate in a proper junction box; twisted-and-taped joints left exposed outside a junction box are flagged as a common, unsafe jobsite shortcut. `confirmed`. [source: yt_VXfpDJruyVs]
- **A bathroom/WC exhaust fan's switch belongs inside the room it serves, not in the adjoining hallway.** Automating a fan is acceptable for auto-OFF (a timer that shuts it off after a delay) but not for auto-ON (an occupancy/humidity sensor that starts it without warning) — the auto-ON case is described as startling/unpleasant for the occupant. `confirmed`, opinion-based but mechanism-specific. [source: yt_KFBS2wgOzrs]
- **Buy finish-stage switches/outlets/frames roughly one week before installation, not months in advance** — reduces storage damage/loss risk and avoids having already bought fixtures invalidated by a mid-project design change. Count the actual number of finish electrical points room-by-room after rough-in is complete (not from the original drawing, since e.g. kitchen point counts commonly shift once final cabinetry is confirmed); keep a small spare stock (roughly 3–4 spare outlets and a few spare switches/frames of each type used, for a ~100 m² apartment) for later breakage/loss. `confirmed`. [source: yt_PIYzs2b4UDU]
- **Prefer spring-clamp (screwless, self-clamping) terminals over screw terminals on finish-stage outlets/switches** — screw terminals are more prone to loosening from thermal cycling over time; evaluate a device by country of manufacture and terminal type rather than by brand name alone. Avoid switches with an integrated LED backlight/indicator (reported flicker/glow issue on some LED bulb types); finalize the actual bulbs being used before buying dimmers, since dimmer compatibility depends on matching leading-edge vs. trailing-edge dimming to the bulb/driver type. `confirmed` as the channel's stated preference. [source: yt_nmVie-r0iis]
- **Place a smart-speaker outlet on the window sill/reveal in every room, including kids' rooms** — extends this channel's window-reveal-outlet habit to a specific new use case: per-room local audio playback (avoids relying on sound carrying from the living room) and correctly room-scoped voice commands (e.g. "close the curtains" resolves to the room the speaker is physically in, not ambiguous apartment-wide). `confirmed`, `single-account`, stated as this company's now-standard practice ("in every modern renovation"). [source: yt_Gp5Lr20SrtU]
- **Add extra outlets in the *upper* portion of a window reveal specifically for hanging holiday garlands/string lights**, in addition to the channel's existing lower-reveal-outlet recommendation — avoids running a visible cord up from a lower outlet to power a garland hung from above. `confirmed`, `single-account`. [source: yt_Gp5Lr20SrtU]

**Lighting design & light source types**

- **Four-category lighting-design framework**: main/general light, task light, decorative light, and night light, each with a distinct recommended color temperature (~4000K for main/task, ~2700–3000K for decorative/night) and a ≥500 lux comfort target for main/task lighting. Night light must be present in hallways and WCs but should **never** be installed in bedrooms (disrupts sleep); decorative lighting is explicitly not a substitute for a dedicated night-light circuit. `confirmed`, coherent taxonomy — the clearest lighting-category framework in this store to date. [source: yt_iRbTlv5Ka9o]
- **Perimeter LED strip lighting (in a stretch-ceiling baguette channel) is decorative/supplementary only** and is never sufficient as a room's main light source, even when a client is told otherwise. Two distinct "main light" fixture types are described: flat light panels (bright, energy-efficient, but difficult to retrofit onto an existing stretch ceiling since they need mounting holes the ceiling wasn't prepped for) and softboxes (fixtures that emit light from their full surface/sides, not just the underside — cited as giving the most even room-fill light at comparatively low wattage, and the channel's own stated preference). `confirmed` as the channel's stated preference and mechanism explanation. [source: yt_GkpOGG_cxM0]

**Rough-in materials, quality checks & hiring red flags**

- **A hired electrician's own tool kit is a usable vetting signal**: a dust-extraction vacuum attached to the drill during wall-chasing/socket-hole cutting is treated as a mandatory red-flag check (its absence means the crew is spreading masonry dust through the unit's cavities, a later air-quality issue); round, cleanly drilled socket-box holes (vs. hacked/oversized square holes) indicate a competent installer. Standard on-center spacing for a ganged multi-socket box run is 71 mm; modern outlets commonly use self-clamping (screwless) terminals. `confirmed`, checkable homeowner-facing signals. [source: yt_XmeoZUiGwBM]
- **Strip cable insulation only with a dedicated wire-stripping tool, never a utility/box knife** — a utility knife risks nicking the conductor's copper strands, a latent failure point. Remove a device's factory-installed mounting screws before rough-finish work (plastering/puttying), not at final finish-electrical install — screw holes left in during finishing get clogged with paint/putty and have to be re-drilled later. `confirmed`, jobsite technique. [source: yt_hqkdbicp994]
- **A window-slope-mounted outlet/switch panel must be glued (foam adhesive) to the slope structure, not merely clipped in** — a loose/wobbly slope outlet when unplugging a cord is a simple, homeowner-testable sign of a bad glue job. The commonly repeated "condensation can short a slope outlet" concern is treated by the channel as a myth: the slope cavity sits at the same temperature as the room, so there is no dew-point crossing inside it to condense water in the first place. `confirmed` as the channel's stated position; `unverified`/physics-reasoning-only, not independently checked here. [source: yt_P7_rUkk8clU]
- **A behind-TV cable channel must be sized at least 40 mm (round or square) at rough-in**, even if the cables planned at the time are thinner — undersized channels can't later accept a bulky HDMI connector body. Behind-TV outlet layout: a 4-module plate with one module deliberately left empty, plus a reserved vertical chase, so a future AV cable can be pulled through after the TV/console setup is finalized. `confirmed`. [source: yt_wWteDpfIso]
- **A country-house intercom (video entry) system needs a long, dedicated cable run pre-planned early** — from the gate/street call panel to the indoor unit — unlike an apartment intercom's short run; retrofitting after the building shell/paving is finished requires demolition. Any outdoor low-voltage cable end (intercom or otherwise) must be sealed against water ingress; an unsealed, untaped cable end was documented on a real jobsite as an active corrosion risk. `confirmed`, real-project observation. [source: yt_1HGrDmukmvc]
- **Plan electrical/room layout against a 5–15 year family-change horizon**, not just the move-in-day household — cited drivers include children aging, handedness, and general life-stage change. A practical future-proofing technique: leave one or two extra outlets wired but hidden behind the finished wallpaper/wall surface, reachable later by cutting the wallpaper rather than opening the wall. Smart-lock-triggered lighting "scenes" are offered as a reconfigurable alternative to a single physical master entrance kill-switch. `confirmed` as the channel's stated design philosophy. [source: yt_k7VUlw8gHZs]
- **Lighting-circuit wiring is thinner-gauge than socket-circuit wiring and is not rated for high-draw loads** — improvising a power tap from a light fixture/socket (e.g. via a bulb-socket adapter) to run a higher-draw tool or appliance risks overheating the lighting circuit's conductors. `confirmed`, safety caveat attached to an otherwise informal "hack" video. [source: yt_C3yZJ_1xL-8]

### Interior Decor / Layout Planning (new topic area, added 2026-08-05)

- **Slat/rail (рейки) wall spacing must equal the light-channel box (короб) module when backlighting is planned** — mismatched spacing makes the backlighting impossible to install after the slats are up. `confirmed`, single-account. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Three slat-to-baseboard junction methods, worst to best for a given project**: (1) run slats flush to the baseboard and cut off — ugly, called injury-risk (mechanism unexplained); (2) baseboard first, cut its back to fit the slats — decent look, simplest, cheapest; (3) slats to the floor before the baseboard, small baseboard pieces fitted between — hardest to execute, most expensive. `confirmed`, single-account. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Wall panels require a leveled wall underneath regardless of finish** — panels mount on guide rails fixed directly to the wall; an unleveled wall produces a visibly warped panel. Called the single biggest, practically unfixable mistake beginners make with wall panels. `confirmed`. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Milled/routed wall panels look more dramatic but are materially harder to keep clean** (especially in the recesses) than smooth solid panels — this channel defaults to smooth panels for comfort-class projects for that reason. `confirmed` as stated practice. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Velour should never be used on any surface (wall/ceiling/floor)** — shows visible marks from the slightest touch, doesn't fully groom out even with repeated brushing. `confirmed` as speaker's strong recommendation, single-account. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Floating furniture (парящая мебель) requires load-rated mounting hardware and design-documented wall prep, even on an already-dense/solid wall** — a common mistake by both installers and designers is failing to specify this explicitly in the design documentation. `confirmed`. [source: yt_1OLVQUJkOdM]
- **Concealed dual-position electrical rough-ins let a room (e.g. a kids' room) be reconfigured later without rewiring**: pre-install outlet points compatible with both a current and a future furniture arrangement at rough-in stage; reconfiguring later needs only cutting the wallpaper over the previously-hidden point and installing the outlet mechanism — the old point doesn't need capping. Underlying electrical-safety mechanism for why capping isn't needed is not explained in the source. `confirmed` as technique; safety rationale `unclear/needs confirmation`, single-account. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **Kitchen/living-room combination should be decided by guest frequency and overnight-guest duration, not defaulted** — contrasting examples: a household with a mother-in-law who sometimes stays a month kept the living room separate/closable as a guest bedroom; a household that rarely has overnight guests combined kitchen+living for more usable space. `confirmed`. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **A windowsill-height desk feature must be verified per-apartment (windowsill height varies unit to unit) and must be paired with blackout/opaque blinds** — prolonged facing of bright/direct light is described as a near-certain eyesight risk, worse on a sun-facing exposure. `confirmed`. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **Washing-machine placement should allow laundry access without entering the bathroom/WC when household laundry frequency is expected to be high** (e.g. a two-child household). `confirmed`. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **Bathroom shower-vs-tub choice should prioritize the primary occupants' stated preference over a guest's**, decided via direct client conversation rather than a default layout — two worked examples given (couple who shower together chosen over mother-in-law's tub preference; elderly couple's difficulty climbing a tub rim led to a shower stall with a fold-down seat). `confirmed`, two real client-conversation-driven decisions. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **Closet/storage allocation should be sized by actual use case, not defaulted to one generic closet**: separate guest-vs-resident storage; gendered/by-gear-type split closets (multi-athlete household); one large dedicated pantry sized so infrequently-used items stay off display but remain quickly retrievable. `confirmed`, three distinct worked examples. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]
- **"The most expensive thing in an apartment isn't expensive materials, it's air"** — an open, low-per-m²-efficiency floor plan is framed as the real cost premium being paid for, not finish quality; speaker explicitly scopes this to one client profile (single, well-earning occupant) rather than general advice. `confirmed` as speaker's own framing, single-account, explicitly non-general. [source: yt_cp8U9wvsqI8]

### Ventilation / Exhaust Duct Concealment (new topic area, added 2026-08-05)

> **Transcript-quality caveat applies to every entry in this subsection**: the source's captions are flagged manually-created (not auto-generated) but the transcript text itself is heavily garbled — treated as `ASR-uncertain` throughout regardless of that flag.

- **Technique for eliminating a bulky exhaust-duct box in a new-build hallway**: demolish the oversized developer duct box, replace with a smaller-cross-section duct; bathroom/toilet exhaust air routes via a forced/booster fan through a round-to-flat adapter, through the hallway, into the building's ventilation shaft; the duct's ceiling junction is pulled tight to the ceiling to reduce kitchen noise; the reworked ducting is concealed behind a stretch ceiling. `single-account`, `ASR-uncertain`. [source: yt_HX2pDdILM7U]
- **Ceiling-height loss from this technique**: total finished ceiling drop ~10 cm, of which ~6 cm is attributed to the developer's own pre-existing duct routing (inherent to the building) and ~4 cm to this technique specifically — framed as practically unnoticeable in most (typically modest-sized) hallways. **The specific cm breakdown is `uncertain`** (one of the most garbled passages in the source); the qualitative claim (net additional loss is small) is better supported than the numbers. [source: yt_HX2pDdILM7U]
- **Duct cross-section trades noise against ceiling drop, and "bigger" isn't "quieter"**: a duct box needs to be thick, not just wide, to cut noise — a wide-but-thin box lowers the ceiling more without the expected noise benefit. Standard cross-section for most systems: 55×110 mm; a powerful exhaust hood needs 250×55 mm instead. Duct length is a separate noise driver (a longer run to the shaft increases noise independent of cross-section). `confirmed`, cleanly-transcribed (unlike the garbled companion source above). [source: yt_ZqfaeREBEYQ]
- **Ceiling drop is typically ~40 mm more than the duct box's own thickness** — the extra allowance is for electrical cable conduit routed alongside/above the duct in the same concealed space; must be pre-calculated in the design project. `confirmed`. [source: yt_ZqfaeREBEYQ]
- **Duct boxes should always get added self-adhesive soundproofing regardless of their own thickness** — a duct is never fully soundproof on its own. Spec: self-adhesive, minimum 3 mm thick. `confirmed`. [source: yt_ZqfaeREBEYQ]
- **Combining natural (passive) and forced (powered) exhaust ventilation in one system creates an inevitable conflict unless a check valve (backflow damper) is installed** — without one, activating forced exhaust pushes air back through the path of least resistance (commonly the natural-vent opening), spreading odors faster rather than removing them. **Second independent source now corroborating this store's existing check-valve/backflow fact** (see `12_Engineering_and_Systems/HVAC_and_Ventilation.md`'s Do's table, previously sourced from two non-Zemstandart kitchen-hood videos). `confirmed`. [source: yt_ZqfaeREBEYQ]

### Design-Project Process & Deliverable Structure (new topic area, added 2026-08-10)

- **An 11-stage design-project delivery process**, from a 5,000 RUB date-reservation deposit through site digitization, technical brief, concept development/approval, 3D modeling/presentation, final drawings, and post-delivery support. Several stages explicitly exclude client participation (concept development, 3D detailing, final drawings) — client involvement is concentrated at specific checkpoints, not continuous. `single-account`, automated-summarization fidelity caveat (see source note). [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **Layered, role-based quality-control review** (surveyor → department head → principal → technologist → QA inspector → project manager → department head again) rather than a single reviewer per stage — the reusable planning idea is the layering itself, independent of this company's specific role titles. `single-account`. [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **Mid-process scope changes should be explicitly bounded, not open-ended** — this company's own policy allows a client to request "additional electrical outlets, but not more" at the 3D-model-presentation checkpoint, a stated narrow change window rather than unlimited revisions. `confirmed` (quoted policy). [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **A reusable 28-item design-project-deliverable checklist** covers room specification, demolition scope, wall/partition plan, opening/portal formation, floor/ceiling plans, tile layout, door/portal schedule, materials calculation sheet, window/slope finishing, balcony/loggia glazing, water-supply/collector scheme, heating/radiator scheme, floor heating, ventilation/AC (incl. a stated back-check-valve recommendation, reinforcing this store's existing check-valve fact above), kitchen module dimensions, electrical general recommendations, switch/emergency-lighting scheme, detailed electrical-point coordinates, furniture dimensions, plus a work-sequence-by-stage section and a basic visualization. **Useful as a planning-completeness audit tool independent of which company is used** — a self-managed renovation could check its own documentation against this list for gaps. `single-account`, self-promotional framing, but the checklist structure itself is a reusable artifact. [source: web_zemspro_about_development]

### Design Technique — Mistakes / Warnings (added 2026-08-10, verbatim-verified via Playwright MCP)

Discovered only via a tab-by-tab real-browser re-check of `web_zemspro_about_development`'s `/about/` page — the original WebFetch summarization pass missed this entire tabbed section (see that source note's fidelity discussion). All four presented by the source as reasons to override client preference — extract the technique, not the "trust us over your own judgment" framing.

- **Drywall ceilings with recessed spotlights are avoided by default**: framed as outdated, prone to cracking/yellowing (repaint cycle ~5-7 years claimed), with recessed spotlights framed as eye-straining, power-hungry, and prone to frequent burnout. `single-account`, specific cycle/burnout claims `unverified`. [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **Frosted/opaque glass toilet doors fail acoustically even when visually opaque** — "everything is audible, as if there's no door at all." A genuinely reusable acoustic-privacy caution, independent of this company. `single-account` but mechanism-specific, not vague. [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **A child's homework desk should not default to the window's left side** — if the light source falls on the same side as the writing hand, the hand shadows the work surface; claimed to contribute to eyesight strain over 1-2 years (`unverified` timeline, but the hand-shadow mechanism itself is concrete and checkable). [source: web_zemspro_about_development]
- **Hygienic-shower/bidet-spray placement should account for user handedness** — for a right-handed user, position it so the right hand washes and the left hand holds the sprayer. `single-account`, concrete ergonomic rule not previously in this store. [source: web_zemspro_about_development]

### Furniture / Built-ins: Wardrobe & Closet Design (new topic area, added 2026-08-10)

> All entries in this subsection come from the same Zemstandart/Zemsproekt channel already established elsewhere in this store as `single-account`/`unverified` per this store's corroboration rule, except where a real client independently corroborates on camera (flagged individually).

- **Storage-zone vertical tiering, single-sided**: 3-tier is the most practical (50cm shoes / rod at 2m / shelf at 210cm); 2-tier-both-clothing and 4-tier variants are flagged as bad (force same-length garments, push rod height to 220-250cm, awkward for shorter people); a 2-tier shoe+clothes variant with the rod at 2m specifically suits rooms with a ducted-ventilation-unit bulkhead eating ceiling height above 210cm. `confirmed`. [source: yt_RyHzFDGgqKA]
- **Zone depth**: clothing 60cm; shoes 35-40cm (max 45cm) — never 60cm for shoes (forces double-row storage that blocks front-row access; shoes should sit perpendicular to the wall, single row). `confirmed`. [source: yt_RyHzFDGgqKA, yt_kj503mBQXq8]
- **Single-sided walk-in closet minimum width: 135cm** (= 10cm trim standoff + 70cm rough opening + 55cm zone depth), reducible to 125cm (no standoff) or 120cm (shortened zone, 30-40cm shelving) with a still-functional 60cm aisle. **Maximum before double-sided applies: 145cm** (150cm allows an opposite 30cm shelf; 175cm+ upgrades to double-sided). `confirmed`, arithmetic-checked. [source: yt_RyHzFDGgqKA]
- **A single-sided walk-in closet wastes space relative to two back-to-back wardrobes sharing a thin dividing wall** — a walk-in's aisle is dead space a plain wardrobe doesn't need; two wardrobes against a shared ~100mm wall between adjoining rooms can add full-depth storage on both sides while also improving inter-room soundproofing. **Real project, client-confirmed on camera**: saved ~700mm of aisle space, lost only 100mm to the dividing wall, gained two full wardrobes plus soundproofing. `confirmed`, real case + client testimonial (stronger evidence tier than most entries here). [source: yt_Xh7uwbKVmfA]
- **Minimum bedroom length to justify carving out a single-sided walk-in without losing other function: 5m** (at 3m room width) — shorter rooms should default to a plain wardrobe instead. `confirmed`, worked example. [source: yt_RyHzFDGgqKA]
- **Three-sided walk-in minimum leg length: 80cm**; storage zones should overlap **counter-clockwise** (right zone placed first, front overlaps it, left overlaps the front) — reasoning: most people are right-handed, so compressing hung clothes with the stronger hand while retrieving with the free hand is more comfortable; the same handedness logic extends to reaching into far shoe-storage corners. `confirmed`, ergonomic handedness rule, distinct from (but complementary to) this batch's other handedness-based rule (hygienic-shower placement, above). [source: yt_RyHzFDGgqKA]
- **Built-in wardrobes are cheaper and more practical than free-standing ("corpus") units**: floor-to-ceiling use is more space-efficient, junction detail is simpler (baseboard + bottom rail only, vs. a full boxed cabinet needing behind-cabinet dust cleaning), and cost is lower (only door panels purchased, not side/back/top/bottom panels). **Independently stated in two sources.** `confirmed`. [source: yt_VgsPDOcPV7c, yt_n0O47DdhATw]
- **Built-in sliding-door (купе) wardrobe sizing**: height = ceiling height by default, split into two independently-doored sections above 280cm room height (main section 230-250cm) since one very tall panel becomes heavy/hard-to-operate and wears its roller track faster; door-panel width 60-90cm optimal (narrower warps/binds, wider is unwieldy); panel height must be 2-4× its width. **Depth — the single most commonly miscalculated figure**: naive assumption is 55cm (everyday)/65cm (outerwear) matching hanging-garment depth alone, but the *correct* minimum is **65-70cm (everyday) / 75-80cm (outerwear, 80cm preferred)** once a 2-3cm air gap per side and the ~10cm sliding-track/rail allowance are added. `confirmed`, explicitly flagged by the source as commonly miscalculated. [source: yt_VgsPDOcPV7c]
- **Entry-zone built-in wardrobe minimum width: 90cm** (80cm wardrobe + 10cm trim), **or 100cm if a light switch also sits on that wall segment** — when the available entry-zone width is undersized, relocate the entrance-door opening rather than accept an undersized wardrobe. `confirmed`. [source: yt_VgsPDOcPV7c]
- **Worked layout for the common ~1.90×1.05m developer storage-room shape**: only one layout is arithmetically workable — 60cm storage zones on both left and right walls with a shallow (~33cm-deep) shoe rack centered at the far end; L-shaped and back-wall-only layouts both fail (leave only 25-45cm for storage after door-opening/aisle allowances). `confirmed`, arithmetic-checked. [source: yt_kj503mBQXq8]
- **A walk-in closet attached to a child's bedroom should be entered from a shared hallway, not from inside the child's own room** — entering from the child's room consumes wall space that room could otherwise use for a proper wardrobe; a closed door already grants the privacy a child might want, regardless of which room the doorway opens from. Side benefit: frees the child's-room wall for a wardrobe with a full-length mirror door. `single-account` (a missed-opportunity case, not a rebuilt one). [source: yt_u28Gqg_V7eE]
- **Technique: recess an entry-zone or room wall inward to create a built-in-wardrobe niche where none exists** — real project recessed 72-78cm, converting an exposed exterior corner into a protected one once boxed with the wardrobe door front. When this interacts with an adjacent balcony/loggia opening, the opening may need repositioning to keep the wardrobe's sliding-door track plane clear (real example: -18cm one side, +34cm the other, preserving an 85cm clear balcony-door opening). `confirmed`, real dimensioned case. [source: yt_n0O47DdhATw]
- **Precompute a wardrobe's sliding-door rail width and wall-junction detail independent of final door-panel placement** — stated as what distinguishes a fully-engineered project from a designer's rendering: every junction/clearance/door-plane variant modeled in advance so nothing needs improvised correction during construction. `confirmed`, general planning-discipline principle. [source: yt_n0O47DdhATw]
- **Bed and TV placement should be locked in before wardrobe placement, not after** — once the two highest-priority fixed elements (bed against the only workable wall, TV opposite it) are set, remaining wall space for storage may require reconfiguring the room's own window/balcony opening. `confirmed`, real 79.5m² project. [source: yt_CHCB4KPupyc]
- **Technique: widen/reposition a window or balcony-block opening to free wall space for a wardrobe** — fully dimensioned worked sequence available in the source note (offsets, threshold height, radiator-niche sizing with a grille-back-mount-vs-decorative-face distinction). `confirmed`, precise dimensioned sequence. [source: yt_CHCB4KPupyc]
- **A mirrored walk-in closet needs a minimum width of 1.7m** (1m mirror-clearance + 60cm clothing zone + 10cm sliding-track allowance) — below that, don't build a walk-in with an internal mirror. **Never place a mirrored wardrobe panel where it would reflect peripheral-vision motion visible from the bed** — real client-confirmed discomfort from a design error (mirrors installed facing the room instead of inside the closet); ~65% of this practitioner's clients reportedly request matte/frosted panels over mirrored ones for this reason (`unverified` self-reported percentage). `confirmed` for the width formula and the real correction; client-corroborated on camera. [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]
- **Scratch-test glass wardrobe-door panels with a fingernail before ordering** — cheap glass shows permanent scratch marks from ordinary door-opening contact; real client-reported defect on installed glass. `confirmed`, real defect + concrete pre-purchase test. [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]
- **Never skip soft-close dampers on heavy glass sliding wardrobe doors** — without them, a forceful slide can shatter the panel against the end stop; a furniture installer pushed back against including them on one project, overridden in favor of keeping them. `confirmed`, real installer-vs-designer disagreement. [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]
- **Never use recessed spotlights inside an enclosed mirrored closet** — even in large numbers, spotlights produce a harsh directional light cone that emphasizes skin/clothing-fold shadows; use a glowing/luminous ceiling panel instead. `confirmed`, real installed mistake + client-reported discomfort. [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]
- **TV height is an inherent compromise, not a mistake with a clean fix**: low enough for comfortable lying-flat viewing looks visually awkward when off (breaks symmetry with flanking millwork); high enough to look intentional forces semi-upright/angled viewing when lying down. No dimension resolves both — a planning caveat to set expectations around, not a rule to "solve." `confirmed`, explicit design-tradeoff framing. [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]
- **Never place a regularly-used work desk/computer setup inside a bedroom** — even quiet keyboard use is reported disruptive to a sleeping partner over repeated regular use; acceptable only as a rarely-used decorative surface, never a daily workstation. Real client corroboration on camera plus a stated recurring-complaint pattern across clients (`single-account` for the recurrence claim, `confirmed` for this client's own experience). [source: yt_8odGOTVAuIk]

### Regulations / Consumer-Protection Process (new topic area, added 2026-08-10, Russia/Moscow-region — NOT routed to the Belarus-specific regulations store, different jurisdiction)

- **Two legal-expertise categories determine what can be claimed in a furniture/contractor dispute**: товароведческая экспертиза (product/goods expertise) for free-standing furniture; строительно-техническая экспертиза (construction-technical expertise) when the item is built-in and its removal affects the apartment's own interior finishing — the latter additionally allows claiming finish-restoration costs. `confirmed`, stated by a named legal professional. [source: yt_pVMvsuhrwWs]
- **The other party must be formally notified of a scheduled expert inspection's date/time in advance** (e.g. via registered telegram) to preserve their documented right to attend — skipping this lets them later contest the expert report's validity in court. `confirmed`, concrete procedural safeguard. [source: yt_pVMvsuhrwWs]
- **An individual entrepreneur (IP) in Russia is personally liable with their own assets** for business debts, and ceasing IP status doesn't extinguish an existing debt (pursuable against the person as a private individual instead). `confirmed` as the lawyer's stated point — relevant when evaluating recourse against a small IP-structured contractor specifically. [source: yt_pVMvsuhrwWs]

### Second Turboscribe Batch (8 sources, added 2026-08-10) — all previously `skipped` for no captions, now processed

All 8 same channel (Zemstandart/Zemsproekt), `single-account`/`unverified` per this store's corroboration rule throughout unless noted otherwise.

**Bathroom / Plumbing:**
- **Never make a bathtub siphon revision-hatch quick-release/magnetic — tile it in and seal the perimeter with silicone.** An unsealed magnetic hatch lets splashed bathwater seep in with no ventilation to dry it out, producing a persistent sewage-like odor with no obvious source; the same water also swells and detaches tile adhesive (hygroscopic), risking tile loss. `confirmed`, two concrete failure mechanisms. [source: yt_3EBbFbhdj6g]
- **Store spare tile, spare grout, and an unopened tube of silicone inside the same sealed bathtub hatch space** — spares stored elsewhere (loggia, storage room, garage) reliably don't survive until needed; tile lines get discontinued and even same-collection replacements differ in color by batch; an already-opened silicone tube dries out before it's needed years later. `confirmed`, practitioner's stated repeated real-world experience. [source: yt_3EBbFbhdj6g]
- **Bathroom room-width formula (tub + one other fixture, risers in an adjacent room)**: 100mm wall thickness + 915mm (15mm tile/adhesive + 100mm left door-trim standoff + 700mm smallest-door allowance + 100mm right standoff) + bathtub width — the 915mm door allowance applies **even if the second fixture is a 600mm washing machine**, since door clearance dominates, not fixture footprint. Do not add the 15mm tile allowance on the tub's own side (tile overhangs the tub rim instead, by design, to keep water from seeping behind it). `confirmed`, arithmetic-checked. [source: yt_SBzDJk_yp8w]
- **Bathtub length must match occupant height** (feet should reach the far wall while lying down, or the occupant slides under water) — rule: tub length ≈ occupant height, or up to 10cm less; 1700mm typical, 1800mm for taller occupants. **This exact rule, and its underlying handedness/height-driven-sizing logic, is independently restated by a second same-channel source as a general planning principle** (client-specific physical factors — height, handedness, mobility — should drive dimensions, not defaults). `confirmed`, worked numeric example given. [sources: yt_SBzDJk_yp8w, yt_z3xJeVPL6n4]
- **Critical mixer-placement mistake**: never center a bathtub's mixer/faucet on the tile-overhung *visible* portion of the tub — always center it on the tub's true physical geometry, since the drain/overflow assembly (a visible reference point) is centered on the real tub, and an off-center mixer next to it reads as visibly wrong. `confirmed`, specific and checkable. [source: yt_SBzDJk_yp8w]
- **Toilet cistern-box height decision rule**: build full floor-to-ceiling with a 600×900mm revision hatch if a water-shutoff manifold or chemical-storage cabinet will sit above the cistern; otherwise build to 1250mm only, topped with a decorative countertop. `confirmed`. [source: yt_SBzDJk_yp8w]
- **Never confuse a vanity/countertop's installation method with a bathtub's**: a tub gets tile overhanging its rim (tile after tub); a vanity/countertop is installed strictly after tiling, with a baseboard/trim strip around its own perimeter instead. `confirmed`, clarifies an existing store distinction. [source: yt_u5wcPgJPDrs]
- **A washing machine positioned under a vanity countertop has a real, often-overlooked ergonomic flaw**: comfortable sink use requires the countertop low enough that elbows sit higher than wrists (so water runs back into the basin) — even the shortest washing machine on the smallest legs raises the countertop too high for this, guaranteeing water drips onto the floor regardless of how the user leans. `confirmed`, non-obvious mechanism, genuinely new. [source: yt_u5wcPgJPDrs]
- **A low-mounted "bottom tap" (floor-level faucet) is a rarely-used but genuinely valuable feature** for washing feet/pet paws, filling buckets, or mopping — valuable for pet-owning or elderly households; must be proactively suggested since most clients don't know it exists. `confirmed`, new fixture-option content. [source: yt_u5wcPgJPDrs]
- **Level/flush bathroom flooring (no threshold) carries real risk beyond its premium look**: reportedly often non-compliant with applicable regulation (`unverified`, no regulation cited, no level-1 location attribution — doesn't qualify for the stricter regulations store) and genuinely leak-exposed even with sensors at every fixture (some water reaches the floor before a sensor trips) — pair only with genuinely waterproof flooring (likely quartz-vinyl/SPC, exact material name `ASR-uncertain` in the transcript). `confirmed` mechanism; `unverified` legal claim. [source: yt_u5wcPgJPDrs]

**Electrical / Lighting:**
- **Rough electrical (cable+connections) service life is distinct from finish electrical (sockets/switches), which depends on user behavior** — cable/connection lifespan does not scale with how carefully a household uses its outlets, unlike socket/switch wear. `confirmed`. [source: yt_NXwfvUwUKLc]
- **Breaker protection provides substantial thermal headroom over a cable's actual rated capacity** — worked example: 2.5mm² cable rated for 20-25A but protected by only a 16A breaker (per this channel's standard pairing, already in this store), meaning the breaker trips well before the cable itself heats up under fault/overload. `confirmed`, explains the mechanism behind this store's existing cable/breaker-pairing table. [source: yt_NXwfvUwUKLc]
- **Stated target: rough electrical should survive a minimum of 5 renovation cycles** (~5-7 years apart), implying a ~30-year minimum lifespan target. `single-account`, self-stated design target. [source: yt_NXwfvUwUKLc]
- **Five distinct arguments against recessed spotlights, demonstrated live with real bulb-failure counts** (extends this store's existing recessed-spotlight warnings): (1) constant bulb burnout — real demonstrated counts (5 of a recently-replaced group already out, 2/9 out elsewhere, 3/12 elsewhere, entire 3-fixture groups dead in an entry hallway); (2) uneven "cone" light pattern makes textured walls look bumpy/uneven; (3) harsh glare when lying down/reclined (bathtub, massage chair), worse with halogen; (4) poor actual room fill-light despite visual brightness — narrow beams hit a dark floor, walls get only cone edges, producing persistent gloominess; (5) higher total cost than one premium fixture once per-fixture wiring, 12V power-supply, drilling, and maintenance are counted — cited as several times the cost of a single high-end chandelier for the same space. **Bonus injury note**: overheated glass diffuser lenses can detach and shatter on the floor (real cited incident). `confirmed`, most thorough single source on this topic in the store. [source: yt_lu1LHG7t9vA]

**Doors / Trim:**
- **Door style should match furniture era**: Modern (default), Hi-Tech (sharp lines/glass, only for genuinely minimalist interiors), Classic (only for >3m ceilings, "imperial" scale looks wrong in typical apartments under ~200m²). `confirmed`. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Casing (наличник) convention for modern doors**: straight/butt-style corner joints, not 45° miters; best-practice detail — the top casing piece slightly thinner than the sides so it sits recessed and hides its own cut end grain. `confirmed`, specific quality signal. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Handle selection**: grip-test (grab firmly ~10× fast; reject if uncomfortable); avoid gold/plated finishes (wear through to bare-metal patches at contact points, prefer silver-toned); avoid oversized/ornate handles. `confirmed`, three concrete criteria. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Hinge type by style**: Hi-Tech = concealed only; Classic = visible only; Modern = either. Concealed-hinge caveat: not all models open a full 180° — specify wide-swing needs (e.g. a bathroom door) explicitly when ordering. `confirmed`. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Prefer silent magnetic door latches over spring-tongue latches** — no protruding mechanism to wear out, engages only on closing. `confirmed`. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Entrance-door sequencing rule**: buy the entrance door first chronologically, but only after interior door style is already decided — the entrance door's interior trim panel must match the (not-yet-installed) interior doors, baseboard, and casing. Correct order: choose interior doors → match baseboard/casing → then select entrance door's interior trim. `confirmed`, non-obvious, easy to get backwards. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]
- **Entrance-door security hardware**: vandal-resistant (non-wood-look) finish; 3 hinges preferred over 2; two locks, one with an armored strike-plate cover, the other re-pinnable (lost keys → new ~2,000 RUB key set, re-pin, old keys stop working, no lock replacement needed); passive deadbolt pins (fixed, hinge-side, prevent grinder-and-swing attacks) recommended; **active deadbolt pins explicitly NOT recommended** — the corners they protect aren't the real weak point, and their linkage rod can wear through and snap, jamming the pin extended and locking the door shut permanently. `confirmed`, real cited failure mechanism for the active-pin caution. [source: yt_ti8J19zY0EM]

**Furniture / Built-ins (extends the existing Wardrobe & Closet Design area):**
- **Free-standing corner-module wardrobes waste ~30% of usable length on the transitional/rounding section alone**, beyond the free-standing cost premium itself — real project figure. [source: yt_UfmUC4-T3jY]
- **A short dividing wall converting an L-shaped zone into two niches lets a built-in wardrobe replace a free-standing one**, using full ceiling height with zero wasted transitional section, and giving the baseboard a correct termination point at a door casing instead of a bare cut edge. `confirmed`, real project (dividing-wall cost: under 2,000 RUB). [source: yt_UfmUC4-T3jY]

**Ceilings (new topic area — no dedicated page content on this specific angle yet):**
- **A stretch ceiling over an L-shaped room section is materially harder and more expensive than two rectangular sections** — a seamless single piece needs an oversized bounding-rectangle sheet with an unreusable offcut; a two-piece L-shaped install produces a long, visible, comparatively short-lived seam. **Splitting via a dividing wall avoids both**, at a real cited cost delta of >10,000-20,000 RUB. Fabric (matte, living spaces) vs. film (glossy, wet rooms) material differences mean offcuts can't cross room types either. `confirmed`, genuinely new for this store's ceiling coverage. [source: yt_UfmUC4-T3jY]
- **Stretch-ceiling fabric has a hard seamless-width limit (added 2026-08-10, standalone video, not part of the second batch above — no prior CSV row existed for it at all)**: manufacturer-rated max 5.10m (5m + 10cm install allowance); practitioner's own planning ceiling is a flat 5m, since real achievable coverage tops out ~5.02-5.03m. **Real cited case**: a combined living-room/kitchen space measuring 5.92m × 8.5m, both dimensions over the limit — fixed by building a false partition wall with a portal opening, splitting the room into two zones each under 5m so each gets its own seamless piece, with the added side-benefit of protecting an exposed exterior corner at the same location (same corner-impact theme already in this store). A distinct mechanism from the L-shaped-ceiling cost penalty above — there the problem was room *shape*, here it's raw *span*. `single-account`, real project, genuinely new hard number for this store's ceiling coverage. [source: yt_2hg0mjR-M30]

**Flooring (new topic area — no dedicated page content on this specific angle yet):**
- **Route flooring transitions through a door opening (terminating at the door frame, not the baseboard) rather than across open floor** — a short transition (real example: 80cm) holds firmly and reads as intentional; a long diagonal seam across an open L-shaped area is both more visible and less durable (longer seams fail faster). `confirmed`. [source: yt_UfmUC4-T3jY]
- **Solid/engineered wood flooring wears unevenly relative to grain direction vs. foot-traffic direction** — durable when boards run parallel to the main walking direction, wears unusually fast when traffic crosses the grain. In an L-shaped corridor this can require different plank directions in each leg, which a dividing wall + door opening accommodates cleanly (transition at the opening) where an open L-shape would force an awkward diagonal seam. `confirmed`, genuinely new material-durability mechanism for this store. [source: yt_UfmUC4-T3jY]
- **Cork flooring is not durable against large-dog claws** — real cited case: a large dog can shred a cork floor within roughly a week of normal use; a client was advised to choose between a cork floor and a large dog, chose the dog, and later thanked the practitioner after seeing a friend's cork floor (installed with a large dog present) reduced to constantly-vacuumed confetti-like debris. `single-account`, real cited case, genuinely new material-selection caution. [source: yt_z3xJeVPL6n4]

**Planning Rules (organizing/cross-cutting):**
- **Explicit renovation dependency chain, tying together several already-recorded facts**: electrical design depends on furniture placement, which depends on corrected room layout, which depends on the client's actual lifestyle/needs, which must be projected ~15 years forward, all further modulated by client-specific physical factors (handedness, height, mobility, weight, pets). Skipping an upstream step guarantees downstream rework. `confirmed`, organizing thesis, not itself new content but a useful explicit statement of how several existing store facts relate. [source: yt_z3xJeVPL6n4]
- **"A well-designed renovation should need no extension cords"**, with exactly one planned exception: a dedicated per-room vacuum-cleaner outlet, placed deliberately. Even seasonal needs (e.g. holiday lights) should have a pre-planned outlet at design stage. `confirmed`, sharpens this store's existing no-extension-cords principle with a named exception. [source: yt_z3xJeVPL6n4]

### Playlist Batch `PLpZakfG8U02ul_ZBCDs1ZQJUnU-XP4gWD` (added 2026-08-10) — 10 fetched via anonymous captions, 2 skipped

**Windows (new topic area at the time — now folded into [[13_Surfaces_and_Finishes/Windows|Windows]], 2026-08-18):**
- **PVC window profile brand has essentially no effect on quality within a market tier (economy/standard/premium)** — profile is just an extruded plastic shape; genuine quality differences exist between tiers, not between brand names within one. Five real quality factors, in stated order of importance: sealing gaskets (must stay elastic for decades), hardware (5-6 locking points from one handle turn), factory assembly quality, double-glazing unit quality, and — ranked most important of all — **installation quality**, without which even a top-tier window will frost/sag/lose its seal within roughly six months. `single-account`, confidently stated, not independently benchmarked. [source: yt_BbtWl3n-GNg]
- **Standard ~1.1m safety-code parapet height conflicts with a panoramic-window design goal** (someone seated sees mostly the opaque parapet, not the view) — the standard resolution is to split the window into a fixed low panoramic pane below that height and a small openable transom (фрамуга) above it, rather than making the whole panoramic section openable. `confirmed`, level 1, filmed demonstration. [source: yt_IAZreuaqhjg]
- **Remote transom-opening hardware (handle + linkage + angled hinge)** lets a high transom, positioned anywhere above a low parapet, be opened from a normally-reachable handle height — avoids the naive alternative's two failure modes: a low handle lets driving rain blow onto the floor through the low opening gap, while a high handle is unreachable without standing on furniture (real risk of falling / breaking the handle off). Includes an integrated anti-break-in push-button lock (a small transom is otherwise easy to force open from outside by pushing), one motion to release from inside. `confirmed`, level 1, mechanism and rationale both stated directly. [source: yt_IAZreuaqhjg]
- **Removable/detachable technical handle with decorative snap-in caps, for a panoramic window section kept normally non-openable for child safety but needing occasional access** (stated example: servicing an external AC condenser unit mounted behind the glazing) — a decorative cap normally sits flush over the handle spindle; sliding it aside exposes the spindle, where a separately-stored ordinary PVC-window technical handle is fitted to open/close the window, then removed and the cap snapped back. Framed as more reliable than a standard child-safety window lock, which the source states parents commonly forget to re-engage after use. Claimed (`single-account`, unverified) to be very difficult for a child to defeat without the stored handle. [source: yt_IAZreuaqhjg]

**Flooring:**
- **Real consumer-dispute case**: quartz-vinyl-adjacent flooring joint separation traced by an independent manufacturer inspection to an incorrect (too-soft, 3mm vs. required ≤2mm-firm) underlayment recommended informally by a retail salesperson — not the "continuous-run installation" cause the retailer first claimed. `single-account` at two removes (client's account of the manufacturer's finding), real cited case. [source: yt_0mrBnaOU3I0]
- **Door-before-laminate sequencing, sharpened with a magnitude claim**: the pool of visually-and-budget-acceptable doors is roughly 100× smaller than the pool of acceptable laminate — fit laminate to doors, never the reverse. **Baseboard immediately follows doors, matched to door color (ideally same manufacturer/collection), not floor color** — floor color is now commonly chosen in deliberate contrast for three reasons (color-match difficulty, inevitable texture mismatch across manufacturers, and a three-way floor/door/furniture match being essentially unachievable). `confirmed`, restates and quantifies this store's existing sequencing theme. [source: yt_PwJsksBs4Ek]
- **Laminate should be milled closer to genuine solid-wood board dimensions (smaller than typical laminate default) to read as convincingly premium**, and **must have a chamfered/beveled edge — flagged as safety-relevant, not just cosmetic**: any laminate eventually gaps between boards; an unchamfered edge leaves the gap sharp-edged, catching cloths/socks and (per the source) risking a lifted-corner trip/cut injury for a barefoot walker. The "dirt collects in the bevel" objection is explicitly debunked as a myth from non-chamfered-product manufacturers. `confirmed`. [source: yt_PwJsksBs4Ek]
- **Material-specific continuous-run-vs-room-separated laying rule, genuinely new decision framework**: solid wood/engineered board must always be laid separately per room (continuous laying guarantees buckling, per this source); ceramic tile should always be laid continuously (doesn't expand with humidity); laminate is the ambiguous case — **the deciding factor is installer/crew reliability and warranty enforceability, not laminate price tier** (the source explicitly reports seeing cheap laminate laid continuously across 150m² with zero issues, and premium laminate that had to be split into rooms after installation — concluding any laminate has roughly even odds of buckling regardless of price). `single-account`, explicitly contradicts the standard trade-recommendation (cheap=separate, premium=continuous) this source itself cites. [source: yt_lOMxNoyW_NE]
- **Laying-direction priority rule**: prioritize the light direction (window-facing rooms have far more area than corridors) over the traffic direction when laying one continuous run across a whole apartment, accepting faster corridor wear as the tradeoff (modern laminate still takes ~7 years to visibly wear there); in a genuinely high-traffic space, lay along the traffic direction instead. Diagonal laying has no real justification (conflicts with both light and traffic direction, wastes material, produces poor doorway offcuts). `confirmed`. [source: yt_lOMxNoyW_NE]
- **Board row-offset pattern: 1/3 of board length is this practitioner's current standard (used since 2015), not the more common 1/2-length "classic"/brick-bond offset** — the 1/2 pattern gives maximum structural bonding but produces a visually repetitive dotted cross-line pattern; the 1/3 pattern spaces cross-joints two rows apart instead of one, breaking up the repetition while still meeting manufacturers' typical ≥1.5×-width cross-joint-separation requirement. Does not apply to ceramic tile (no structural interlock). `single-account`, own-practice decision. [source: yt_lOMxNoyW_NE]
- **Glue-down (non-locking) quartz-vinyl installation — the most detailed single installation-technique source in this store**: acclimate 2-3 days at the room's actual future operating temperature (not just "indoors") before laying, store packs flat never on edge, never glue to a gypsum self-leveling floor, prime substrate even if visually dust-free (2 coats for a two-component primer), verify moisture ≤5% and flatness (2mm max deviation over a 2m straightedge), install at 18-21°C never above 24°C, shuffle planks across boxes/batches before laying (batch color varies), inspect both faces and all corners of every plank, tape down the first laid planks, never set tools directly on the fresh floor, save and reuse offcuts (near-zero waste, unlike click-lock product), and don't walk on it for 48h after installation. `single-account`, extensively detailed, not cross-checked against a manufacturer guide. [source: yt__VvT9FcNbKY]
- **Chain-hypermarket flooring "special series" spec-downgrade scam**: manufacturers produce a lower-spec product line under identical branding/packaging specifically for large chain retailers, priced 1.5-2× cheaper than the same-looking product at a specialized retailer — the downgrade (e.g. wear-class 31 vs. 33) is sometimes undisclosed on packaging and only detectable by close spec comparison. Real cited consequences: laminate swelling from two drops of water, odorous/fast-wearing quartz vinyl, engineered board warping at 1.2% humidity swing. `single-account`, plausible given known general retail-tier-differentiation practices, not independently verified for this specific claim. [source: yt_XFhz1NXlln8]

**Walls:**
- **Gas-concrete-block wall material-quantity formula**: 7 blocks per m² (any thickness), 1 adhesive bag per m³ regardless of block thickness (thinner blocks need more units but proportionally less adhesive per unit) — apply a real-world +20-25% correction over the nominal manufacturer figure. First course always on cement-sand mortar (levels the uneven raw floor), never block adhesive, which is a thin-joint system. **Never buy the full calculated quantity up front** — order roughly half first, since a second purchase is unavoidable once openings/cuts are accounted for. Project documentation should show per-wall labeled area, not a lump total (which only supports a plastering-area calculation, no other material calc). `single-account`, own-practice figures. [source: yt_8kOUv9EVQTQ]
- **Only 2 of 6 commonly-used gas-concrete-block bonding materials are actually correct, per a real destructive-test comparison with a guest technologist**: cement-sand mix (first course only, never beyond — ~5× the material use of specialized adhesive, poor plasticity); Rotband/gypsum plaster (technically bonds, but needlessly expensive at its required 5mm minimum thickness, and risks a chemistry clash if the room is later finished with cement-based plaster in a humid space); tile adhesive (bonds fine, but ~50% cost premium for unneeded tile-specific additives — negligible on one wall, tens of thousands of RUB at full-project scale); **specialized mineral block adhesive — correct default** (thin-joint, -10°C to +35°C rated, one product covers the full seasonal range); **specialized polyurethane "foam-glue" — this source's top recommendation** (clean, no water/electricity needed, ~3× faster, 30min/24h strength development vs. 3-day/28-day for mixes — caveat: first course must be leveled with extreme precision since foam-glue can't correct subsequent rows the way mortar can); **ordinary construction/mounting foam — explicitly, categorically wrong** despite visual similarity to the specialized foam-glue, since its secondary expansion pushes blocks out of alignment. `single-account`/guest-affiliated (Bonolit), real filmed test. [source: yt_p2nyMFY9x-0]
- **Drywall false-wall framing masterclass (added 2026-08-10, general technique)**: laser-plane-builder layout method for a wall running parallel to an existing one; use guide-track profile throughout (not stud profile) to avoid needing connector clips; join elements with a professional crimping tool, not screws (screws are a myth for added strength, and every screw head locally deforms the drywall face); brace to the main wall with fabricated angle brackets, re-checking verticality throughout; cut drywall sheets in place rather than by tape-measure-and-pencil. Includes a specific convector-niche framing method (10cm clearance above, niche 5mm oversized vs. the grille) directly relevant to a loggia's balcony-block lower section. `single-account`. [source: yt_Y-eITaok1Gw]

**Bathroom / Electrical (extends existing heated-floor coverage in `Bathroom_Guide.md` §24 with thermostat *placement*, previously uncovered):**
- **Underfloor-heating thermostat must always be visibly mounted, never hidden** (unlike a light switch, there's no other cue of on/off/temperature state). A loggia should always get underfloor heating regardless of insulation quality (the unit below is very likely unheated); its thermostat goes on the loggia door's own reveal — specifically the side visible from the connected bedroom's bed — never behind the entry curtain or inside the loggia itself. A bathroom/WC thermostat always goes **outside** the room. **General heuristic: place the thermostat on the same side (in/out of the room) as that room's own light switch.** Mount at eye level (160-170cm), not waist height or floor level — the stated reason is LCD legibility (only readable head-on), not child-safety (which the source explicitly argues is not the real justification for height). Recommends an "electro-mechanical" type (LCD display + physical buttons) over fully mechanical (no state readout) or fully touchscreen (sluggish, over-featured — ~98% of clients use only one function). **Always extend the sensor wire by soldering** (the one place in modern finish electrical still recommending solder, since the joint must repeatedly pass through a dedicated replacement conduit as sensors — unlike heating elements — do eventually fail) — install that conduit from thermostat to sensor pocket at rough-in stage. `single-account`, own-practice figures. [source: yt_uwXBHuWPUIo]

### Balcony / Loggia — Playlist Batch `PLpZakfG8U02uIUNU8GbIPnvkY04AjoaGC` (13 loggia-topic sources, added 2026-08-10)

All sources below are Alexey Zemskov / Zemstandart-Zemsproekt (Moscow), `single-account` throughout unless noted, spanning 2018-2024 — treat repeated claims across sources as one channel's consistent convention over time, not independent corroboration. Full detail in each linked extraction note; this section condenses to the durable, reusable rules.

**Should you join a loggia at all — the decision this whole batch sits under, and a real tension with this project's own plan:**
- **Glazing/insulation alone never makes a loggia warm without an active heat source** — a well-glazed loggia still equalizes toward outdoor temperature without heating. For a kept-separate (unjoined) loggia, thin-film underfloor heating is recommended over a convector/radiator, since the floor is the coldest surface. `confirmed` mechanism. [source: yt_aoKerAFdZwg]
- **A real, easy-to-fall-into failure mode for a loggia kept unjoined but regularly propped open for warmth**: the door blocks fridge/curtain/switch access, and — more seriously — a glazed door left permanently open deforms on its hinges within ~6 months (its full weight shifts from the frame to the hinges) and typically stops closing at all, often getting removed entirely. **This is a real risk specifically if the actual day-to-day plan for an unjoined loggia involves habitually propping the door open rather than a genuinely separate, properly-closing loggia.** [source: yt_aoKerAFdZwg]
- **Joining trades real benefits (shared central-heating warmth, merged countertop, more light) against real costs**: a joined room runs ~3-4°C colder than the rest of the apartment (real problem for a cold-sensitive occupant or a bedroom/kids' room), brings more street noise, and is always technically unregistrable — though the practical inspection risk differs sharply by whether the original opening was widened (see below). **Unconditional never-join case**: a loggia with continuous facade glazing shared across the building's full height — cold aluminum profile, can't be insulated at the slab junction within reasonable budget. [source: yt_aoKerAFdZwg] *(Note: `yt_gXC77Y_QIbc` shows a real project that appears to be exactly this "unfixable" case, worked around via a full skeleton-only rebuild — a possible tension with the unconditional rule above, not resolved here.)*
- **If NOT joining: never remove the sill wall under the balcony-block door/window to install a second clear door for "more light"** — the loggia's own opaque parapet blocks nearly all the light gain anyway, while removing the wall forces a real, expensive, hard-to-legitimize radiator relocation with zero benefit. [source: yt_XllES56exkk]
- **If thinning the apartment-side wall to gain interior width (up to ~30cm): only do this if the loggia will genuinely be insulated and heated afterward** — otherwise the thinned wall becomes a direct cold bridge into the apartment (condensation, mold), since the cold/warm boundary must always face outward. **Directly relevant to this project's own stated plan** (currently unheated) — this technique should not be applied unless that plan changes. [source: yt_oIED8ehn2Is]
- **Inspection-cost reality, a genuinely quantified real-world data point**: joining without altering the original opening configuration is cheap to "legalize" on paper (install any cheap frame back in the unaltered opening, ~10,000 RUB signing fee — inspectors work from a floor plan only, no wall-elevation drawing); joining *with* a widened opening requires full reversal before any signing, priced from ~200,000 RUB. Mirroring a balcony-block's door side needs no approval at all, but only with underfloor (not standard riser) heating. [source: yt_XllES56exkk]

**Glazing installation — planning through acceptance, the fullest technical coverage in this store to date:**
- **Joint-tolerance and glazing spec floor**: min. foam joints 20mm sides/30mm top/15-40mm bottom (base-dependent); 70mm frame profile, ≥40mm glazing unit, quality gaskets/hardware (≥5 strike points), no jamb-extension profiles needed around the perimeter. [source: yt_dvKPBN_kpRI]
- **Sash-count minimization**: sales commission scales with order size, creating a structural incentive to oversell opening sashes — minimize sashes to the fewest needed for cleaning access and cross-ventilation. **Sectioning**: max ~3m per fabricated/transportable unit; optimal section width 65-75cm. [source: yt_dvKPBN_kpRI]
- **The single highest-value technical rule in this batch**: align the new frame's *inner* face flush with the parapet's inner plane, not the outer plane — outer alignment creates an unfixable cold bridge at the sill junction; inner alignment lets the later insulation layer neutralize the weak point. [source: yt_dvKPBN_kpRI]
- **Support-masonry construction** (raising the parapet to accept new glazing): position by the frame's own fixing points (avoiding slab rebar), lay every course thin-joint directly following the base's true unevenness (never level by varying adhesive thickness — a common, slow, structurally worse mistake), trim only the top course to level after dry-fitting, reinforce the *center* span (not the edges) with a tensioned metal strap. Drip-cap slope: raise masonry ~1cm above the old railing per 5cm of future drip-cap width, measured from the railing's highest point. [source: yt_cRRwnezXMcw]
- **Reclaiming floor width**: removing the old railing and building masonry in its place (not alongside it) can reclaim up to ~1m² on a 6m loggia — real load calc shows this is never a structural concern (~66kg/m² vs. ~800kg/m² slab rating). Masonry and siding must interleave course-by-course, not be built separately. **All of this requires professional demolition with fall-arrest equipment**, unlike the rest of the process. [source: yt_jxrd7pZTkvI]
- **Full installation sequence** (assembly → demolition → install → drip-cap → glazing → vapor barrier) and a **complete customer acceptance checklist** (tools, tolerances — 1.5mm/m max 3mm total for plumb, no foam voids, every locking point functional, drip-cap sound test, profile/hardware/glass markings vs. spec) are both fully documented — the checklist source is the single most directly actionable content in this batch for a self-managed buyer verifying a hired crew's work. [sources: yt_BuIwpW6mWl8, yt_tPyMPPVNvqY, yt_irOVnHty0fc]
- **Floor-to-ceiling/panoramic-specific**: fall-arrest harness required; glaze *before* foaming (large constructions can deform under foam expansion otherwise); bottom section spec'd in triplex safety glass (room-facing) plus frosted glass for privacy where floor-to-ceiling. [source: yt_tPyMPPVNvqY]
- **Real emergency case, safety-relevant**: a brick parapet's structural integrity secretly depended entirely on the old glazing and a few cold-bridging metal brackets — discoverable with a simple push/kick test before any demolition begins. Repair requires welded steel bracket reinforcement with **compression-type anchors specifically, never standard window-frame anchors** (mixing the two undoes the whole repair). Worth checking on any existing parapet before demolition, including this project's own. [source: yt_f39c0NtE-28]

**Finishing and technique:**
- **PVC panel cladding**: seamless corner-forming (cut the back rib, heat-bend), retrofit heavy-item mounting via a protruding dowel bushing, pre-attaching outlets to panels before installation, and a fast grille-disguised cosmetic repair for scratches/holes. [source: yt_aABAJy6-kdM]
- **Insulated subfloor construction** (directly relevant to this project's plan): height governed by 3 hard constraints (step ≤20cm, ceiling ≥2.45m, railing ≥95cm/110cm with young children) plus a 15cm-minimum baseboard-clearance reason; framing/leveling/insulation/sheathing sequence fully documented, including the "downstairs neighbor's insulation doesn't warm your floor" myth-debunk and the final perimeter-sealing step (prevents wool-fiber migration into the room via the ventilated under-panel cavity). [source: yt_1f8dQOhEADk]
- **Wide-sill and lighting rules**: max-width sill at an angled corner (not a narrow one) to make the space usable; never a visible sill splice; loggia lighting must be dedicated (never borrowed from the adjoining room) and never ceiling-mounted (blocks sash opening) without a minimum 100mm jamb extension. [source: yt_i2hIpC70mKQ]
- **Real cross-cutting case**: splitting one uselessly-deep (83cm) closet into two properly-usable (40cm each) compartments shared across a kitchen/loggia wall boundary — reinforces this store's existing "no kitchen has too much storage" theme with a second real project. [source: yt_Qjt47tedKqw]

### Balcony / Loggia — Radiator Relocation, Installation & Inspection-Evasion (Playlist `PLpZakfG8U02uR3KlsxtWXyChOETu-o-Aj`, added 2026-08-14)

- **Radiator relocation onto a joined loggia carries real, cited inspection consequences, now described with concrete operational detail**: this store already records (`yt_aoKerAFdZwg`) that moving a central-heating radiator onto a loggia is illegal, with real fines and a mandated 3-day restoration order. This source describes, in the same practitioner's own direct account, what an inspection actually finds and orders (loggia's heating removed, work redone) and — the standout new fact — **the company's routine practice of temporarily concealing the violation**: swapping in the cheapest available plastic balcony block before an expected inspection (satisfying a surface-level check that works from a floor plan, not a physical teardown) and swapping the real radiator connection back in afterward. Stated as standard service for clients who want a joined/heated loggia without registering it, not a one-off anecdote. **Corroborated within-channel** by `yt_uUnTk_qVoa4` (#216, 2023-06-18), which describes essentially the same scheme ~2 weeks earlier — same-practitioner repetition, not independent corroboration, but consistent across two separate videos. `confirmed` (source's own direct, detailed description), `single-account` at the corroboration level. [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]
- **Radiator connector supply/return orientation is a real, checkable installation-quality risk**: reversing which port carries supply vs. return causes a thermostatic radiator valve (TRV) to malfunction (radiator runs constantly too hot or too cold) even though the radiator still "heats" in a basic sense — not visually obvious after installation. Factory plugs mark the ports (red=supply, blue=return); installers additionally tag them with colored tape once plugs are removed, since the bare pipe ends look identical. Relevant to any radiator installation, not loggia-specific. [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]
- **Radiator clearance requirement for convector-style units**: 150-200mm combined clearance above+below is needed for proper convection — a real constraint on niche/countertop height whenever a radiator sits below a desk-height or counter-height surface (a real project here chose a 350mm center-to-center radiator specifically to fit a 75cm desk-height countertop above it). [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]
- **Radiator floor-bracket ("туфли") technique**: cut to length and positioned *in situ* by physical test-fit only, never pre-calculated from wall measurements (a common beginner mistake that routinely comes out slightly wrong and requires rework); brackets mount to the radiator's second-from-edge section on each side. Never fasten bracket anchors directly into aerated concrete even for an apparently one-off fixing — normal radiator servicing (removal for painting, accidental knocks) will pull the screw out over time; always use the pre-drilled, supplied anchors. A decorative cap is meant to cover the finished/plastered fastener assembly — a frequently-skipped finishing step that leaves visible screw heads. [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]
- **Plumbing-pipe-cutting rule**: cut connecting pipe only with a dedicated pipe cutter, never an angle grinder — a grinder cut leaves a burr unsuitable for a compression/press-fit connection and will leak; the correct tool also chamfers the outer edge, requiring a separate manual deburring pass for the inner edge (check both by touch). Cut connecting pipe lengths with margin first, test-fit, then trim the final amount — pipe can be shortened but never lengthened. [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]
- **Does not clear this store's stricter regulations-store bar**: no location is named in the video itself (channel-level Moscow association only) — stays in this main store, not `renovation_regulations_belarus_knowledge_store.md`. [source: yt_IzsMSjOUKmc]

### Kitchen / Kitchen Appliances (new topic area, added 2026-08-14)

- **Corner-kitchen-into-recessed-wall defect mechanism**: when a developer's layout has one wall recessed (real example: ~720mm) relative to an adjacent wall, and plumbing stacks/utilities constrain the kitchen run to one specific segment, forcing a corner-kitchen configuration into that segment produces a narrow entry passage with the kitchen's own protruding external corner right at the doorway — that corner then takes constant foot-traffic wear (wallpaper or paint) and needs frequent refinishing. `single-account`, but a concrete, checkable mechanism, not a vague "corners are bad" caution. [source: yt_gvNczaG3SLY]
- **Verify every door-swing option before committing to a layout, not just the first one considered**: a real example shows all three swing directions for one doorway failing for different reasons (visual protrusion past a corner, conflict with an adjacent door, blocking a WC door entirely) — the general lesson is to check all swing options against the full room, not just clearance at the door itself. [source: yt_gvNczaG3SLY]
- **Convector/radiator grille clearance is a real corner-sofa selection constraint**: when choosing a corner sofa for a room with a wall-mounted convector, verify the specific sofa model doesn't block the convector's grille — a specific, checkable furniture/HVAC interaction, not a generic caution. [source: yt_gvNczaG3SLY]
- **Food waste disposer — what NOT to put in is counter-intuitive**: fibrous produce (real jam-failure case: banana peel, vegetable trimmings) winds around the cutting mechanism and stops it working — the manufacturer's own instructions also prohibit pumpkin, asparagus, and artichoke leaves, alongside the general no-plastic rule. Hard items (bones) are explicitly fine; soft fibrous items are the actual hazard, contrary to a first-time user's own initial assumption. `single-account` (client testimony), real cited failure case. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Food waste disposer — what CAN safely go in**: chicken/rabbit bones, grape seeds, bread, cheese, soup, coffee grounds, even a fish head. `single-account`. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Food waste disposer hand-safety rule**: never push food waste in by hand — use the dedicated pusher tool, since the unit's blades are active during operation. `single-account`, safety-relevant. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Food waste disposer vs. sewage lift pump ("сололифт") — a genuinely useful disambiguation**: a disposer only activates on a user button-press, never automatically; a сололифт (a separate device that pumps sewage to a level the natural drain slope can't reach) activates automatically via its own sensor/float. The two devices are commonly confused by first-time buyers. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Food waste disposer electrical-safety claim**: speaker and a real client both state the water+electricity combination inside a disposer cannot deliver a shock. `unverified`/single-account — no technical mechanism (e.g. sealed motor housing) given, just an assurance; recorded as a claim, not adopted as a verified engineering fact. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Food waste disposer maintenance and noise**: effectively self-cleaning (running water during grinding flushes internal components, no separate user maintenance needed); does produce audible grinding noise but it's partially masked by the running water and brief (well under a minute per use). Physical footprint roughly comparable to a 5-liter water bottle, compact enough to typically still leave room for a separate under-sink water filter. `single-account`. [source: yt_9vcDyi4utLk]
- **Built-in refrigerator — a "standard" built-in is never large; a large built-in fridge is by definition a non-standard unit** (example given: 750mm width) — offered as settling a common point of buyer confusion. `single-account`. [source: yt_7VfZIYGUrTo]
- **Built-in refrigerator — always choose the version with an integrated freezer compartment over a fresh-zone-only unit**, per this company's own repeated real-project experience that clients who skip the freezer regret it and later need a bolted-on separate freezer module. `single-account`, backed by one specific cited real-project consequence. [source: yt_7VfZIYGUrTo]
- **Standalone ice generators need a dedicated water-supply line** — they do not make ice from air; commonly forgotten when planning rough plumbing. `single-account`. [source: yt_7VfZIYGUrTo]
- **Refrigerator-cabinetry interior panels must be special-ordered pre-colored on all sides from the factory if matching a custom facade color — never repainted post-fabrication.** Real cited company case: standard white-interior modules were repainted blue afterward to match a custom facade, and the paint later peeled, forcing a full module rebuild. `confirmed`, this company's own real rework case. [source: yt_7VfZIYGUrTo]
- **Touch-open/sensor-front refrigerator facades will always show fingerprints, regardless of surface finish (matte/gloss/soft-touch)** — mitigate by always pairing a touch-open unit with a milled pull handle on adjacent cabinetry, so a user with dirty/greasy hands never has to touch the fridge's own facade directly. `single-account`, but a specific, checkable design-mitigation pairing. [source: yt_7VfZIYGUrTo]

### Planning Rules / Layout Critique — Zemstandart Layout-Critique Videos (new topic area, added 2026-08-14)

- **Wet rooms must be squared/rectangular regardless of the shell's own geometry, with no exceptions** — an irregular building shell (trapezoidal, curved, acute/obtuse corners) may have its irregularity "absorbed" into circulation space (a corridor) instead, but the bathroom/WC itself must never contain an acute or obtuse corner. Stated twice for emphasis as an absolute rule, not a preference. `confirmed`, `single-account`. [source: yt_RF6FdHk_jgE]
- **Size a utility-concealment box (false wall around plumbing/electrical) for the full hardware assembly it must contain, not the minimum needed to hide the pipes** — flagged explicitly as a common beginner-designer mistake: shrinking the box to pipe-clearance minimums leaves no room for the actual water-intake manifold or panel hardware it needs to house. `confirmed`, `single-account`, but a clear general design principle independent of the specific project. [source: yt_RF6FdHk_jgE]
- **Build false walls to the full thickness of a structural protrusion (e.g. a column), never leave a small protrusion unresolved and wallpaper over it** — stated as a firm rule ("must never be done" for the sloppy version), specifically because an uneven wall surface is both visually and functionally wrong; the same false-wall technique doubles as a way to conceal heating radiators while correcting an uneven exterior wall in the same intervention. `confirmed`, `single-account`. [source: yt_RF6FdHk_jgE]
- **Provision a bedroom TV outlet pair + coax cable even when the client says a TV isn't wanted there, specifically for resale reasons** — this practitioner's stated standing advice: an apartment without pre-wired bedroom-TV provisioning reportedly becomes a common price-negotiation point for buyers at resale, so pre-wiring (left unterminated behind wallpaper if unused) avoids that risk even when the current occupant has no plan to use it. `confirmed` as the speaker's stated practice, `unverified` as a market-wide resale-negotiation claim.
